# Turkey

This article is about the country. For the bird, see [Turkey (bird)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey_\(bird\)). For other uses, see [Turkey (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey_\(disambiguation\)).

"Türkiye" redirects here. For other uses, see [Türkiye (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%BCrkiye_\(disambiguation\)).

**Turkey**,[^cite_note-11] officially the **Republic of Türkiye**,[^cite_note-12] is a country mainly located in [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) in [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia), with a smaller part called [East Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Thrace) in [Southeast Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Europe). It borders the [Black Sea](3386.md) to the north; [Georgia](48768.md), [Armenia](10918072.md), [Azerbaijan](746.md), and [Iran](14653.md) to the east; [Iraq](7515928.md), [Syria](7515849.md), and the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) to the south; and the [Aegean Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Sea), [Greece](12108.md), and [Bulgaria](3415.md) to the west. Turkey is home to over 86 million people;[^cite_note-Population_of_Turkey-7] most are ethnic [Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_people), while [Kurds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurds_in_Turkey) are the [largest ethnic minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minorities_in_Turkey).[^cite_note-cia-4] Officially [a secular state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism_in_Turkey), Turkey has [a Muslim-majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Turkey) population. [Ankara](802.md) is Turkey's capital and [second-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_cities_and_towns_in_Turkey) city. [Istanbul](3391396.md) is its largest city and economic center. Other major cities include [İzmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir), [Bursa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bursa), and [Antalya](361527.md).&#x20;

First inhabited by modern humans during the [Late Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Paleolithic),[^cite_note-Howard2016p24-13] present-day Turkey was home to [various ancient peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ancient_peoples_of_Anatolia).[^cite_note-14] The [Hattians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hattians) were assimilated by the [Hittites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hittites) and other [Anatolian peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_peoples).[^cite_note-15] [Classical Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Anatolia) transitioned into cultural [Hellenization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenization) after [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great)'s conquests, and later [Romanization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Anatolia) during the [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) and [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine) eras.[^cite_note-16] The [Seljuk Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seljuk_Empire) began migrating into Anatolia in the 11th century, starting the [Turkification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkification) process.[^cite_note-17] The Seljuk [Sultanate of Rum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Rum) ruled Anatolia until the [Mongol invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasions_of_Anatolia) in 1243, when it disintegrated into [Turkish principalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_beyliks).[^cite_note-Howard_2016_38–39-18] Beginning in 1299, the [Ottomans](22278.md) united the principalities and [expanded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rise_of_the_Ottoman_Empire). [Mehmed II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_II) conquered [Constantinople (modern-day Istanbul) in 1453](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople). During the reigns of [Selim I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selim_I) and [Suleiman the Magnificent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suleiman_the_Magnificent), the Ottoman Empire became a [global power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_power).[^cite_note-19] From 1789 onwards, the empire saw major changes, [reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanzimat), centralization, and [rising nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rise_of_nationalism_in_the_Ottoman_Empire) while [its territory declined](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decline_and_modernization_of_the_Ottoman_Empire).[^cite_note-20]&#x20;

In the 19th and early 20th centuries, [persecution of Muslims during the Ottoman contraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Muslims_during_the_Ottoman_contraction) and [in the Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Muslims#Russian_Empire) resulted in large-scale loss of life and [mass migration into modern-day Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhacir) from the [Balkans](4829.md), [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus), and [Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Khanate).[^cite_note-21] Under the control of the [Three Pashas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Pashas), the Ottoman Empire [entered World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_entry_into_World_War_I) in 1914, during which the Ottoman government committed [genocides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Ottoman_genocides) against its [Armenian](140376.md), [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_genocide), and [Assyrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_genocide) subjects.[^cite_note-Tatz-22][^cite_note-SchallerZimmerer-23][^cite_note-24] Following Ottoman defeat, the [Turkish War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_War_of_Independence) resulted in the [abolition of the sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolition_of_the_Ottoman_sultanate) and the signing of the [Treaty of Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Lausanne). Turkey emerged as a more homogenous [nation state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_state).[^cite_note-25] The Republic [was proclaimed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_the_Republic_of_Turkey) on 29 October 1923, modelled on [the reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atat%C3%BCrk%27s_reforms) initiated by its founder and first president, [Mustafa Kemal Atatürk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Kemal_Atat%C3%BCrk). Turkey [remained neutral during most of World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey_during_World_War_II), but was involved in the [Korean War](16772.md). Several military interventions interfered with the transition to a multi-party system.&#x20;

Turkey is an [upper-middle-income](1242956.md) and [emerging country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerging_country); [its economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Turkey) is the world's [16th-largest by nominal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) and [11th-largest by PPP-adjusted GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)). As the [15th-largest electricity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_electricity_production) producer in the world, Turkey aims to become a hub for regional energy transportation. It is a [unitary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state) presidential [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_system). Turkey is a founding member of the [OECD](33853117.md), [G20](2206608.md), and [Organization of Turkic States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Turkic_States). With a geopolitically significant location, Turkey is a [NATO](21133.md) member and has [its second-largest military personnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_states_of_NATO#Military_personnel). It may be recognized as an [emerging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerging_power), a [middle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power), and a [regional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_power) power. As [an EU candidate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accession_of_Turkey_to_the_European_Union), Turkey is part of the [EU Customs Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EU_Customs_Union).&#x20;

Turkey has coastal plains, [a high central plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Turkey), and various mountain ranges with rising elevation eastwards. [Turkey's climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Turkey) is diverse, ranging from [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_climate) and other [temperate climates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_climates) to [semi-arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-arid) and [continental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_climate) types. Home to three [biodiversity hotspots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_hotspot), Turkey is prone to [frequent earthquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_earthquakes_in_Turkey) and is [highly vulnerable to climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Turkey).[^cite_note-Birben_2019-26][^cite_note-Ahmed_2006_pp_1575_1576-27][^cite_note-World_Bank_climate_change-28] Turkey has [a universal healthcare system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_in_Turkey), growing [access to education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Turkey), and increasing levels of [innovativeness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index).[^cite_note-njm-29][^cite_note-OECDp35-30][^cite_note-31] It is a leading [TV content](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_television_drama) exporter.[^cite_note-32] With numerous UNESCO [World Heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Turkey) sites and [intangible cultural heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intangible_cultural_heritage) inscriptions, and [a rich and diverse cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_cuisine), Turkey is the [fourth most visited country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Tourism_rankings) in the world.&#x20;

## Etymology

Main articles: [Name of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_Turkey) and [Turkic peoples § Etymology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples#Etymology)

|  | This section **contains an excessive amount of intricate [detail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:DETAIL)**. Please help [improve it](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Turkey\&action=edit) by [spinning off](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Content_forks#Article_spinoffs:_.22Summary_style.22_meta-articles_and_summary_sections) or [relocating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Handling_trivia#Recommendations_for_handling_trivia) relevant information and removing excessive detail that goes against [Wikipedia's inclusion policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:What_Wikipedia_is_not). *(May 2026)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

*Turchia*, meaning "the land of the Turks", had begun to be used in European texts for [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) by the end of the 12th century.[^cite_note-33][^cite_note-34][^cite_note-Everett-35] As a word in [Turkic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_languages), *Turk* may mean "strong, strength, ripe" or "flourishing, in full strength".[^cite_note-36] It may also mean ripe as in for a fruit or "in the prime of life, young, and vigorous" for a person.[^cite_note-37] As an [ethnonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnonym), the etymology is still unknown.[^cite_note-38] In addition to usage in languages such as Chinese in the 6th century,[^cite_note-Everett-35] the earliest mention of *Turk* (𐱅𐰇𐰺𐰜, *türü̲k̲*; or 𐱅𐰇𐰼𐰚, *türk/tẄrk*) in Turkic languages comes from the [Second Turkic Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Turkic_Khaganate).[^cite_note-39]&#x20;

In [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine) sources in the 10th century, the name *[Tourkia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourkia_\(disambiguation\))* was used for defining two medieval states: [Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Hungary) (*Western Tourkia*); and [Khazaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourkia_\(Khazaria\)) (*Eastern Tourkia*).[^cite_note-constantine_vii-40][^cite_note-41] The [Mamluk Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_Sultanate), with its ruling elite of Turkic origin, was called the "State of the Turks" (*Dawlat at-Turk*, or *Dawlat al-Atrāk*, or *Dawlat-at-Turkiyya*).[^cite_note-42] Turkestan, also meaning the "land of the Turks", was used for a historic region in [Central Asia](6742.md).[^cite_note-43]&#x20;

[Middle English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_English) usage of *Turkye* or *Turkeye* is found in *[The Book of the Duchess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Book_of_the_Duchess)* (written in 1369–1372) to refer to Anatolia or the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md).[^cite_note-44] The modern spelling *Turkey* dates back to at least 1719.[^cite_note-45] The [bird called turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey_\(bird\)) was named as such due to trade of [guineafowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guineafowl) from Turkey to England.[^cite_note-Everett-35] By the mid-19th century, the name *Turkey* was sometimes used in referring to international treaties of the Ottoman Empire.[^cite_note-46] In 1920, the [Government of the Grand National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_the_Grand_National_Assembly) adopted the name *Türkiye Devleti* ("State of Turkey").[^cite_note-47] With the [Treaty of Alexandropol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Alexandropol) of December 1920, the name *Türkiye* entered international documents for the first time. In the treaty signed with [Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Afghanistan) in 1921, the expression *Devlet-i Âliyye-i Türkiyye* ("Sublime Turkish State") was used, likened to the Ottoman Empire's name.[^cite_note-48]&#x20;

Upon the [proclamation of the republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_the_Republic_of_Turkey) on 29 October 1923, the name *Türkiye Cumhuriyeti* (توركيه جمهوريتى) was adopted. In December 2021, President [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan) called for expanded official usage of *Türkiye*, saying that *Türkiye* "represents and expresses the culture, civilization, and values of the Turkish nation in the best way".[^cite_note-Genelge-2021/24-49] In May 2022, the Turkish government requested the [United Nations](31769.md) and other international organizations to use *Republic of Türkiye* officially in English; the UN agreed.[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52] Until 2022, its official English name remained the *Republic of Turkey*. Though not official, the term *Turkish Republic* is sometimes used in descriptive, historic, or academic contexts.&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of Turkey](28451535.md)

See also: [History of Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Anatolia), [History of Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Thrace), and [Ancient regions of Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_regions_of_Anatolia)



Some [henges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henge) at [Göbekli Tepe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%B6bekli_Tepe) were erected as far back as [9600 BC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%B6bekli_Tepe#Large_enclosures), predating those of [Stonehenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonehenge) by over seven millennia.

[^cite_note-ArchMag-53]

### Prehistory and ancient history

Main articles: [Prehistory of Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory_of_Anatolia) and [Prehistory of Southeast Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory_of_Southeast_Europe)

See also: [Hattians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hattians), [Hittites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hittites), [Luwians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luwians), and [Pala (Anatolia)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pala_\(Anatolia\))



Map of the Hittite Empire at its greatest extent, with Hittite rule c. 1300 BC

Present-day Turkey has been inhabited by [modern humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_humans) since the [late Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Paleolithic) period and contains some of the world's oldest [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) sites.[^cite_note-Howard2016p24-13][^cite_note-MET-54] [Göbekli Tepe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%B6bekli_Tepe) is close to 12,000 years old.[^cite_note-Howard2016p24-13] Parts of [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) include the [Fertile Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertile_Crescent), an [origin of agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origin_of_agriculture).[^cite_note-55] Other important Anatolian Neolithic sites include [Çatalhöyük](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%87atalh%C3%B6y%C3%BCk) and [Alaca Höyük](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaca_H%C3%B6y%C3%BCk).[^cite_note-Howard_2016-3-56] Neolithic Anatolian farmers differed genetically from farmers in [Iran](14653.md) and [Jordan Valley](12105764.md).[^cite_note-Bellwood_2022-57] These early Anatolian farmers also [migrated into Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_European_Farmers), starting around 9,000 years ago.[^cite_note-Bellwood_2022-57][^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59] [Troy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troy)'s earliest layers go back to around 4500 BC.[^cite_note-Howard_2016-3-56]&#x20;

Anatolia's historical records start with [clay tablets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clay_tablets) from approximately around 2000 BC that were found in modern-day [Kültepe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%BCltepe).[^cite_note-Howard_2016-4-60] These tablets belonged to an [Assyrian trade colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karum_\(trade_post\)).[^cite_note-Howard_2016-4-60] The languages in Anatolia at that time included Hattian, Hurrian, [Hittite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hittite_language), [Luwian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luwian), and [Palaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palaic).[^cite_note-McMahon_Steadman_2012_p_234-61] [Hattian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hattic_language) was a language indigenous to Anatolia, with no known modern-day connections.[^cite_note-McMahon_Steadman_2012_p_234-61][^cite_note-62] [Hurrian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurrian_language) was used in northern [Syria](7515849.md).[^cite_note-McMahon_Steadman_2012_p_234-61] Hittite, Palaic, and Luwian languages were "the oldest written [Indo-European languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages)",[^cite_note-63] forming the [Anatolian sub-group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_languages).[^cite_note-McMahon_Steadman_2012_p_522-64][^cite_note-68]&#x20;

[Hattian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hattians) rulers were gradually replaced by [Hittite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hittites) rulers.[^cite_note-Howard_2016-4-60] The Hittite kingdom was a large kingdom in Central Anatolia, with its capital of [Hattusa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hattusa).[^cite_note-Howard_2016-4-60] It co-existed in Anatolia with [Palaians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pala_\(Anatolia\)) and [Luwians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luwians), approximately between 1700 and 1200 BC.[^cite_note-Howard_2016-4-60] As the Hittite kingdom was disintegrating, further waves of Indo-European peoples migrated from southeastern Europe, which was followed by warfare.[^cite_note-69] The [Thracians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thracians) were also present in modern-day [Turkish Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Thrace).[^cite_note-Ahmed_2006-70] It is not known if the [Trojan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trojan_War) is based on historical events.[^cite_note-71] [Troy's Late Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Bronze_Age_Troy) layers matches most with *[Iliad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iliad)*'s story.[^cite_note-72]&#x20;

### Early classical antiquity

Main article: [Classical Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Anatolia)

See also: [Phrygia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phrygia), [Lydia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lydia), [Lycia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lycia), [Caria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caria), [Urartu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urartu), [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire), [Hellenization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenization), and [Hellenistic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_period)



The [Lycian Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lycian_Way) is a 760 kilometers (470 mi) long hiking path in Southwestern Turkey.

[^cite_note-73]

Around 750 BC, [Phrygia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phrygia) had been established, with its two centers in [Gordium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gordium) and modern-day [Kayseri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kayseri).[^cite_note-Howard_2016-2-74] [Phrygians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phrygians) spoke an Indo-European language, which was closer to [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) than Anatolian languages.[^cite_note-McMahon_Steadman_2012_p_522-64] Phrygians shared Anatolia with [Neo-Hittites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Hittites) and [Urartu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urartu). Luwian-speakers were probably the majority in various Anatolian Neo-Hittite states.[^cite_note-75] Urartians spoke a non-Indo-European language and their capital was around [Lake Van](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Van).[^cite_note-76][^cite_note-Howard_2016-2-74] Urartu and Phrygia fell in seventh century BC.[^cite_note-Howard_2016-2-74][^cite_note-Howard_2016-77] They were replaced by [Carians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carians), [Lycians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lycians) and [Lydians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lydians).[^cite_note-Howard_2016-77] These three cultures "can be considered a reassertion of the ancient, indigenous culture of the Hattian cities of Anatolia".[^cite_note-Howard_2016-77]&#x20;

In the late [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age), there were three[^cite_note-Bryce_2012_pp369–370-78] or four[^cite_note-Britannica-79] Greek-speaking settlements in [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia), including [Miletus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miletus).[^cite_note-Bryce_2012_pp369–370-78][^cite_note-Britannica-79] Around 1000 BC, [Greeks started migrating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age_Greek_migrations) to the west coast of Anatolia.[^cite_note-Howard_2016-2-74] These eastern Greek settlements played a vital role in shaping the Archaic Greek civilization;[^cite_note-Howard_2016-2-74][^cite_note-80] important [cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polis) included [Miletus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miletus), [Ephesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ephesus), [Halicarnassus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halicarnassus), [Smyrna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smyrna) (now [İzmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir)) and [Byzantium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantium) (now [Istanbul](3391396.md)).[^cite_note-81] These settlements were grouped as [Aeolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeolis), [Ionia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionia), and [Doris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doric_hexapolis), after the specific Greek groups that settled them.[^cite_note-82][^cite_note-83] Further Greek colonization in Anatolia was led by Miletus and [Megara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megara) in 750–480 BC.[^cite_note-84]&#x20;



The [Library of Celsus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_Celsus) in [Ephesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ephesus) was built by the [Romans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) in 114–117.

[^cite_note-85]

[Cyrus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_the_Great) attacked eastern Anatolia in 547 BC, and [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire) eventually [expanded into western Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Sardis_\(547_BC\)).[^cite_note-Howard_2016-77] In the east, the [Armenian province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satrapy_of_Armenia) was part of the Achaemenid Empire.[^cite_note-Howard_2016-2-74] Following the [Greco-Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Persian_Wars), the Greek city-states of the Anatolian Aegean coast regained independence, but most of the interior stayed part of the Achaemenid Empire.[^cite_note-Howard_2016-77] Two of the [Seven Wonders of the Ancient World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Wonders_of_the_Ancient_World), the [Temple of Artemis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_of_Artemis) in Ephesus, and the [Mausoleum of Halicarnassus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mausoleum_of_Halicarnassus), were located in Anatolia.[^cite_note-86]&#x20;

Following the victories of Alexander in [334 BC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Granicus) and [333 BC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Issus), the Achaemenid Empire collapsed.[^cite_note-Howard_2016-77] This led to increasing cultural homogeneity and [Hellenization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenization) of Anatolia,[^cite_note-87][^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89] which met resistance in some places.[^cite_note-Howard_2016_a-90] Following Alexander's death, the [Seleucids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucids) ruled large parts of Anatolia, while native Anatolian states emerged in the Marmara and Black Sea areas. In eastern Anatolia, [the kingdom of Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Armenia_\(antiquity\)) appeared.[^cite_note-91] In third century BC, [Celts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celts) invaded central Anatolia and continued as a major ethnic group in the area for around 200 years. They were known as the [Galatians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galatians_\(people\)).[^cite_note-92]&#x20;

### Rome and Byzantine Empire

Main articles: [Classical Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Anatolia) and [Byzantine Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Anatolia)

See also: [Roman Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Republic), [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), [Romanization of Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Anatolia), [Christianity in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Turkey), and [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire)



The [Eastern Roman (Byzantine) Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) in 555 under [Justinian the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justinian_the_Great), at its greatest extent

When [Pergamon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Pergamon) requested assistance in its conflict with the Seleucids, [Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Republic) intervened in Anatolia in the second century BC. Without an heir, Pergamum's king left the kingdom to Rome, which was annexed as [province of Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Asia). Roman influence grew in Anatolia afterwards.[^cite_note-93] Following [Asiatic Vespers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asiatic_Vespers) massacre, and [Mithridatic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mithridatic_Wars) with [Pontus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Pontus), Rome emerged victorious. Around the 1st century BC, [Rome expanded into parts of Pontus and Bithynia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_province), while turning rest of Anatolian states into Roman satellites.[^cite_note-94] Several [conflicts with Parthians ensued](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman%E2%80%93Parthian_Wars), with peace and wars alternating.[^cite_note-95]&#x20;

According to [Acts of the Apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_the_Apostles), early Christian Church had significant growth in Anatolia because of [St Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Paul)'s efforts. Letters from St. Paul in Anatolia comprise the [oldest Christian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christianity).[^cite_note-Howard_2016-5-96] Under Roman authority, [ecumenical councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_councils) such as [Council of Nicaea (Iznik)](11118.md) in 325 served as a guide for developing "orthodox expressions of basic Christian teachings".[^cite_note-Howard_2016-5-96]&#x20;



The [Hagia Sophia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia) in [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) (now [Istanbul](3391396.md)) was built by the [Eastern Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Roman) emperor [Justinian the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justinian_the_Great) in 532–537.

[^cite_note-97]

The Byzantine Empire, also referred to as the Eastern Roman Empire, was the continuation of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) centered in [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) during [Late Antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Antiquity) and the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages). The eastern half of the Empire survived the conditions that caused the [fall of the West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Western_Roman_Empire) in the 5th century AD, and continued to exist until the [fall of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople) to the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) in 1453. During most of its existence, the empire remained the most powerful economic, cultural, and military force in the [Mediterranean world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_world). The term *Byzantine Empire* was only coined following the empire's demise; its citizens referred to the polity as the "Roman Empire" and to themselves as *Romans*. Due to the imperial seat's move from Rome to [Byzantium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantium), the [adoption of Christianity as the state religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_as_the_Roman_state_religion), and the predominance of [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Greek) instead of [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin), modern historians continue to make a distinction between the earlier *Roman Empire* and the later *Byzantine Empire*.&#x20;

In the early Byzantine Empire period, the Anatolian coastal areas were Greek speaking. In addition to natives, interior Anatolia had diverse groups such as [Goths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goths), [Celts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celts), [Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persians) and [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_the_Byzantine_Empire). Interior Anatolia had been "heavily Hellenized".[^cite_note-Horrocks_pp_778-779-98] [Anatolian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_languages) eventually became extinct after [Hellenization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenization) of Anatolia.[^cite_note-99]&#x20;

### Seljuks and Anatolian beyliks

Main articles: [Seljuk Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seljuk_Empire), [Sultanate of Rum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Rum), and [Anatolian beyliks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_beyliks)

Further information: [Turkic migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_migration), [Oghuz Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oghuz_Turks), and [Turkification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkification)

According to historians and linguists, the [Proto-Turkic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Turkic_language) originated in Central-East Asia.[^cite_note-Uchiyama_et_al_2020-100] Initially, Proto-[Turkic speakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_languages) were potentially both hunter-gatherers and farmers; they later became [nomadic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomadic) [pastoralists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastoralists).[^cite_note-Uchiyama_et_al_2020-100] Early and medieval [Turkic groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples) exhibited a wide range of both East Asian and West-Eurasian physical appearances and genetic origins, in part through long-term contact with neighboring peoples such as [Iranic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranic), [Mongolic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongolic_peoples), [Tocharian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tocharians), [Uralic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uralic_people), and [Yeniseian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeniseian_people) peoples.[^cite_note-101] During the 9th and 10th centuries CE, [the Oghuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oghuz_Turks) were a Turkic group that lived in the [Caspian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic%E2%80%93Caspian_steppe) and [Aral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aral_Sea) steppes.[^cite_note-Lee_p84-102] Partly due to pressure from the [Kipchaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kipchaks), the Oghuz migrated into [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Plateau) and [Transoxiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transoxiana).[^cite_note-Lee_p84-102] They mixed with Iranic-speaking groups in the area and converted to [Islam](6037917.md).[^cite_note-Lee_p84-102] Oghuz Turks were also known as Turkmen or [Turkoman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkoman_\(ethnonym\)).[^cite_note-Howard_2016_34-103][^cite_note-Lee_p84-102]&#x20;



A map of independent [Turkish principalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_beyliks) in Anatolia during the 14th century

The ruling family of [the Seljuks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seljuq_dynasty) originated from the *[Kınık](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%B1n%C4%B1k_\(tribe\))* branch of the Oghuz Turks.[^cite_note-104] In 1040, the Seljuks defeated the [Ghaznavids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghaznavids) at the [Battle of Dandanaqan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Dandanaqan) and established the [Seljuk Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seljuk_Empire) in [Greater Khorasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Khorasan).[^cite_note-105] [Baghdad](4492.md), the [Abbasid Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate)'s capital and center of [the Islamic world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age), was taken by Seljuks in 1055.[^cite_note-Howard_2016_34-103] Given the role [Khurasani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khurasani) traditions played in art, culture, and political traditions in the empire, the Seljuk period is described as a mixture of "[Turkish, Persian and Islamic influences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turko-Persian_tradition)".[^cite_note-106] In the latter half of the 11th century, the Seljuk Turks began penetrating into [medieval Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Armenia) and Anatolia.[^cite_note-Howard_2016_34-103] At the time, Anatolia was a diverse and largely Greek-speaking region after previously being [Hellenized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenized).[^cite_note-Ahmed_2006_p1576_h-107][^cite_note-Davison_1990_p3_4-108][^cite_note-Horrocks_pp_778-779-98]&#x20;

The Seljuk Turks defeated the Byzantines at the [Battle of Manzikert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manzikert) in 1071, and later established [the Seljuk Sultanate of Rum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Rum).[^cite_note-109] During this period, there were also [Turkish principalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_beyliks) such as [Danishmendids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danishmendids).[^cite_note-110] Seljuk arrival started the [Turkification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkification) process in Anatolia;[^cite_note-Davison_1990_p3_4-108][^cite_note-Ahmed_2006-70] there were Turkic/Turkish migrations, intermarriages, and conversions into Islam.[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112] The shift took several centuries and happened gradually.[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114] Members of [Islamic mysticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mysticism#Islamic_mysticism) orders, such as [Mevlevi Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mevlevi_Order), played a role in the [Islamization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization) of the diverse people of Anatolia.[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116] Seljuk expansion was one of the reasons for the [Crusades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades).[^cite_note-117] In 13th century, there was a second significant wave of Turkic migration, as people fled [Mongol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire) expansion.[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119] Seljuk sultanate was defeated by the Mongols at the [Battle of Köse Dağ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_K%C3%B6se_Da%C4%9F) in 1243 and disappeared by the beginning of the 14th century. It was replaced by various Turkish principalities.[^cite_note-Howard_2016_38–39-18][^cite_note-120]&#x20;

### Ottoman Empire

Main article: [Ottoman Empire](22278.md)



The [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) at its greatest European extent, in 1683, during the [Battle of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Vienna)

Based around [Söğüt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%B6%C4%9F%C3%BCt), [Ottoman Beylik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Beylik) was founded by [Osman I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osman_I) in the early 14th century.[^cite_note-121] According to Ottoman chroniclers, Osman descended from the *[Kayı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kay%C4%B1_\(tribe\))* tribe of the [Oghuz Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oghuz_Turks).[^cite_note-Lee_2023_94-122] Ottomans started annexing the nearby Turkish beyliks (principalities) in Anatolia and expanded into the [Balkans](4829.md).[^cite_note-123] [Mehmed II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_II) completed Ottoman conquest of the Byzantine Empire by [capturing its capital, Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople), on 29 May 1453.[^cite_note-124] [Selim I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selim_I) united Anatolia under Ottoman rule.[^cite_note-Howard_p45-125] Turkification continued as Ottomans mixed with various indigenous people in Anatolia and the Balkans.[^cite_note-Lee_2023_94-122]&#x20;

The Ottoman Empire was a global power during the reigns of [Selim I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selim_I) and [Suleiman the Magnificent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suleiman_the_Magnificent).[^cite_note-Howard_p45-125][^cite_note-Somel_p_xcvii-126] In the 16th and 17th centuries, [Sephardic Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_the_Ottoman_Empire) moved into Ottoman Empire following their [expulsion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconquista#Conversions_and_expulsions) from Spain.[^cite_note-127] From the second half of the 18th century onwards, the [Ottoman Empire began to decline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decline_of_the_Ottoman_Empire). The [Tanzimat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanzimat) reforms, initiated by [Mahmud II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_II) in 1839, aimed to modernize the Ottoman state in line with the progress that had been made in Western Europe. The [Ottoman constitution of 1876](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_constitution_of_1876) was the first among Muslim states, but was [short-lived](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Constitutional_Era).[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129]&#x20;





[Topkapı Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topkap%C4%B1_Palace) and [Dolmabahçe Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolmabah%C3%A7e_Palace) were the primary residences of the [Ottoman sultans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_sultans) in [Istanbul](3391396.md) between 1465 and 1856

[^cite_note-nytimes-130]

&#x20;and 1856 to 1922,

[^cite_note-dolmabahcepalace-131]

&#x20;respectively.

As the empire gradually shrank in size, military power and wealth; especially after the [Ottoman economic crisis and default](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Eastern_Crisis) in 1875[^cite_note-NiallFergusonFT-132] which led to uprisings in the Balkan provinces that culminated in the [Russo-Turkish War (1877–1878)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Turkish_War_\(1877%E2%80%931878\)). The decline of the Ottoman Empire led to a [rise in nationalist sentiment among its various subject peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rise_of_nationalism_under_the_Ottoman_Empire), leading to increased ethnic tensions which occasionally burst into violence, such as the [Hamidian massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamidian_massacres) of [Armenians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenians_in_the_Ottoman_Empire), which claimed up to 300,000 lives.[^cite_note-nzhistory.net.nz-133] Ottoman territories in Europe ([Rumelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumelia)) were lost in the [First Balkan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Balkan_War) (1912–1913).[^cite_note-134] Ottomans managed to recover some territory in Europe, such as [Edirne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edirne), in the [Second Balkan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Balkan_War) (1913).&#x20;

In the 19th and early 20th centuries, [persecution of Muslims during the Ottoman contraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Muslims_during_the_Ottoman_contraction) and [in the Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Muslims#Russian_Empire) resulted in estimated 5 million deaths,[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-Gibney_2005_437-136] with the casualties including Turks.[^cite_note-Gibney_2005_437-136] Five to seven or seven to nine million [refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhacir) migrated into modern-day Turkey from the [Balkans](4829.md), [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus), [Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Khanate), and [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean) islands,[^cite_note-137] shifting the center of the Ottoman Empire to Anatolia.[^cite_note-138] In addition to a small number of Jews, the refugees were overwhelmingly Muslim; they were both Turkish and non-Turkish people, such as [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians) and [Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Tatars).[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140] [Paul Mojzes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Mojzes) has called the Balkan Wars an "unrecognized genocide", where multiple sides were both victims and perpetrators.[^cite_note-141] Circassian refugees included the survivors of the [Circassian genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassian_genocide).[^cite_note-142]&#x20;

Following the [1913 coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1913_Ottoman_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat), the [Three Pashas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Pashas) took control of the Ottoman government. The Ottoman Empire entered [World War I](4764461.md) on the side of the [Central Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Powers) and was ultimately defeated.[^cite_note-143] During the war, the empire's Armenian subjects were [deported to Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temporary_Law_of_Deportation) as part of the [Armenian genocide](140376.md). As a result, an estimated 600,000[^cite_note-britannica-ag-144] to more than 1 million,[^cite_note-britannica-ag-144] or up to 1.5 million[^cite_note-umichigan-145][^cite_note-jfreedman-146][^cite_note-totten-et-al-147] [Armenians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenians_in_the_Ottoman_Empire) were killed. The Turkish government has [refused to acknowledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_genocide_denial)[^cite_note-Tatz-22][^cite_note-148] the events as genocide and states that Armenians were only ["relocated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_transfer)" from the eastern war zone.[^cite_note-149] [Genocidal campaigns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Ottoman_genocides) were also committed against the empire's other minority groups such as the [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_genocide) and [Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_genocide).[^cite_note-Bloxham2005-150][^cite_note-Levene-151][^cite_note-Ferguson-152] Following the [Armistice of Mudros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armistice_of_Mudros) in 1918, the victorious [Allied Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I) sought the [partition of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) through the 1920 [Treaty of Sèvres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_S%C3%A8vres).[^cite_note-Treaty_of_Sèvres-153]&#x20;

### Turkish Republic

Main article: [History of the Republic of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Republic_of_Turkey)



[Mustafa Kemal Atatürk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Kemal_Atat%C3%BCrk), the founder and the [first president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_Turkey) of the Turkish Republic

The [occupation of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Istanbul) (1918) and [İzmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Smyrna) (1919) by the [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I) in the aftermath of World War I initiated the [Turkish National Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_National_Movement). Under the leadership of [Mustafa Kemal Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Kemal_Pasha), a military commander who had distinguished himself during the [Battle of Gallipoli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Gallipoli), the [Turkish War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_War_of_Independence) (1919–1923) was waged with the aim of revoking the terms of the [Treaty of Sèvres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_S%C3%A8vres) (1920).[^cite_note-Atatürk-154]&#x20;

The [Turkish Provisional Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Provisional_Government) in [Ankara](802.md), which had declared itself the legitimate government of the country on [23 April 1920](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_National_Assembly_of_Turkey), started to formalize the legal transition from the old Ottoman into the new Republican political system. The Ankara Government engaged in armed and diplomatic struggle. In 1921–1923, the Armenian, Greek, French, and British armies had been expelled.[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157][^cite_note-18sep1922-158] The military advance and diplomatic success of the Ankara Government resulted in the signing of the [Armistice of Mudanya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armistice_of_Mudanya) on 11 October 1922. On 1 November 1922, the Turkish Parliament in Ankara formally abolished the Sultanate, thus ending 623 years of [monarchical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchical) Ottoman rule.&#x20;

The [Treaty of Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Lausanne) of 24 July 1923, which superseded the Treaty of Sèvres,[^cite_note-Treaty_of_Sèvres-153][^cite_note-Atatürk-154] led to the international recognition of the sovereignty of the Turkish state as the successor state of the Ottoman Empire. On 4 October 1923, the Allied occupation of Turkey ended with the withdrawal of the last Allied troops from [Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Istanbul). The Turkish Republic [was officially proclaimed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_the_Republic_of_Turkey) on 29 October 1923 in Ankara, the country's new capital.[^cite_note-159] The [Lausanne Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_Concerning_the_Exchange_of_Greek_and_Turkish_Populations) stipulated a [population exchange between Greece and Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_exchange_between_Greece_and_Turkey).[^cite_note-Clogg-160]&#x20;

Mustafa Kemal became the republic's first [president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Turkey) and introduced [many reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atat%C3%BCrk%27s_reforms). The reforms aimed to transform the old [religion-based and multi-communal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet_\(Ottoman_Empire\)) Ottoman monarchy into a Turkish [nation state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_state) that would be governed as a [parliamentary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_republic) under a [secular constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Constitution_of_1924).[^cite_note-BoweringCrone2012-161] Women gained the right to vote nationally in 1934.[^cite_note-162] With the [Surname Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surname_Law), the [Turkish Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Parliament) bestowed upon Kemal the honorific surname "Atatürk" (*Father Turk*).[^cite_note-Atatürk-154] Atatürk's reforms caused discontent in some [Kurdish](17068.md) and [Zaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zazas) tribes leading to the [Sheikh Said rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Said_rebellion) in 1925[^cite_note-163] and the [Dersim rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dersim_rebellion) in 1937.[^cite_note-164]&#x20;

[İsmet İnönü](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0smet_%C4%B0n%C3%B6n%C3%BC) became the country's second president following Atatürk's death in 1938. In 1939, the [Republic of Hatay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Hatay) voted in favor of joining Turkey with a referendum. Turkey [remained neutral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Cairo_Conference) during almost all of [World War II](32927.md),[^cite_note-165] but entered the war on the side of the [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II) on [23 February 1945](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_declaration_of_war_on_Germany_and_Japan).[^cite_note-166] Later that year, Turkey became a [charter member](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Charter) of the United Nations.[^cite_note-Turkey_UN-167] In 1950 Turkey became a member of the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe). After fighting as part of the [UN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN) forces in the [Korean War](16772.md), Turkey joined [NATO](21133.md) in 1952, becoming a bulwark against Soviet expansion into the [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_Basin).&#x20;



[Tansu Çiller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tansu_%C3%87iller), Turkey's first female prime minister, attends a [European Commission](9974.md) meeting in January 1994.

Military coups or memorandums, which happened in [1960](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_Turkish_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat), [1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Turkish_military_memorandum), [1980](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Turkish_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat), and [1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Turkish_military_memorandum), complicated Turkey's transition to a democratic [multiparty system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-party_period_of_the_Republic_of_Turkey).[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169] Between 1960 and the end of the 20th century, the prominent leaders in Turkish politics who achieved multiple election victories were [Süleyman Demirel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%BCleyman_Demirel), [Bülent Ecevit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%BClent_Ecevit) and [Turgut Özal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turgut_%C3%96zal). PKK started a "campaign of terrorist attacks on civilian and military targets" in the 1980s.[^cite_note-170] It is designated as a [terrorist organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorist_organization) by Turkey,[^cite_note-Ministry_Foreign_Affairs_statement-171] the United States,[^cite_note-department-of-state-list-172] and the European Union.[^cite_note-european-council-list-173] [Tansu Çiller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tansu_%C3%87iller) became the first female prime minister of Turkey in 1993. Turkey applied for full membership of the [EEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EEC) in 1987, joined the [European Union Customs Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union_Customs_Union) in 1995 and started [accession negotiations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accession_of_Turkey_to_the_European_Union) with the [European Union](9317.md) in 2005.[^cite_note-TR_EUChrono-174][^cite_note-175] Customs Union had an important impact on the Turkish manufacturing sector.[^cite_note-176][^cite_note-177]&#x20;

Since 2002, Turkey has been governed by successive governments led by [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan)'s [Justice and Development Party](403264.md) (AKP). The party has faced criticism over issues such as increasing centralization of political authority.[^cite_note-178] One of the most prominent examples of these criticisms was the [Gezi Park protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gezi_Park_protests) in 2013. The demonstrations emerged in response to plans to demolish Istanbul's [Gezi Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gezi_Park) and construct a shopping mall on the site, as well as broader environmental concerns, but subsequently expanded to include protests over what demonstrators described as [democratic backsliding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding_in_Turkey) and increasing government control.[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180] More than a dozen people were killed, many as a result of [police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_brutality) [brutality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_brutality).[^cite_note-\:1-181]&#x20;

In 2014, prime minister Recep Tayyip Erdoğan won Turkey's first direct [presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Turkish_presidential_election).[^cite_note-182] On 15 July 2016, an [unsuccessful coup attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Turkish_coup_attempt) tried to oust the government.[^cite_note-183] According to the Turkish government, there are 13,251 arrested or convicted people in jail as of 2024, related to the 2016 coup attempt.[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185] With a [referendum in 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Turkish_constitutional_referendum), the parliamentary republic was replaced by an [executive presidential system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_president). The office of the prime minister was abolished, and its powers and duties were transferred to the president. On the referendum day, while the voting was still underway, the [Supreme Electoral Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Election_Council_\(Turkey\)) lifted a rule that required each ballot to have an official stamp.[^cite_note-WasPostRef2017-186] The opposition parties claimed that as many as 2.5 million [ballots without a stamp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Turkish_constitutional_referendum#Unstamped_ballots) were accepted as valid.[^cite_note-WasPostRef2017-186] In 2025 the PKK declared a ceasefire.[^cite_note-187]&#x20;

In March 2025, the [detention and arrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrest_of_Ekrem_%C4%B0mamo%C4%9Flu) of Istanbul mayor and [CHP](83257098.md) presidential candidate [Ekrem İmamoğlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ekrem_%C4%B0mamo%C4%9Flu), along with around 100 other people, was described by the Associated Press as a "dramatic escalation in a crackdown on the opposition and dissenting voices in Turkey."[^cite_note-188] These measures sparked [nationwide protests](79502864.md), the largest in Turkey since the 2013 Gezi Park demonstrations, during which 1,879 people were detained.[^cite_note-189] Later, in May 2026 ,the Ankara appeals court [declared](83257098.md) the CHP primaries' results as void. The court decision removed CHP chairman [Özgür Özel](49050282.md) and reinstated former leader [Kemal Kılıçdaroğlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemal_K%C4%B1l%C4%B1%C3%A7daro%C4%9Flu) on an interim basis, a ruling criticized by [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) as a major blow to "rule of law, democracy, and human rights".[^cite_note-190]&#x20;

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Turkey)

See also: [List of national parks of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_parks_of_Turkey)



[Topographic map](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topographic_map) of Turkey

Turkey covers an area of 783,562 square kilometres (302,535 square miles).[^cite_note-191] With [Turkish straits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_straits) and [Sea of Marmara](28863.md) in between, Turkey bridges [Western Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Asia) and [Southeastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Europe).[^cite_note-Kuzucuoğlu_2019_p7-192] Turkey's Asian side covers 97% of its surface, and is often called [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia).[^cite_note-anatolia_definition-193] Another definition of Anatolia's eastern boundary is an imprecise line from the [Black Sea](3386.md) to [Gulf of Iskenderun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Iskenderun).[^cite_note-194] [Eastern Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Thrace), Turkey's European side, includes around 15% of the population and covers 3% of the surface area.[^cite_note-195] The country is encircled by seas on three sides: the [Aegean Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Sea) to the west, the Black Sea to the north and the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) to the south.[^cite_note-Waskey_2005_922-196] Turkey is bordered by Georgia, Armenia, Azerbaijan and Iran to the east.[^cite_note-Waskey_2005_922-196] To the south, it's bordered by Syria and Iraq.[^cite_note-197] To the north, its Thracian area is bordered by Greece and Bulgaria.[^cite_note-Waskey_2005_922-196]&#x20;

Turkey is divided into "[seven major regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographical_regions_of_Turkey)": [Marmara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marmara_Region), [Aegean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Region), [Central Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Anatolia), [Black Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea_Region), [Eastern Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Anatolia), [Southeastern Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Anatolia) and the [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_Region,_Turkey).[^cite_note-Waskey_2005_922-196] As a general trend, the inland [Anatolian Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_Plateau) becomes increasingly rugged as it progresses eastward.[^cite_note-TRGeo_TRMinistryTourism-198] Mountain ranges include [Köroğlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ro%C4%9Flu_Mountains) and [Pontic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic_Mountains) mountain ranges to the north, and the [Taurus Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taurus_Mountains) to the south. The [Lakes Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Lakes_Region) contains some of the largest lakes in Turkey such as [Lake Beyşehir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Bey%C5%9Fehir) and [Lake Eğirdir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_E%C4%9Firdir).&#x20;



Tectonic map of Turkey.

[^cite_note-199]

&#x20;Straight lines and lines with triangles denote different types of [faults](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fault_\(geology\)), such as [North Anatolian Fault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Anatolian_Fault) and [East Anatolian Fault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Anatolian_Fault).

Geographers have used the eastern Anatolian plateau, Iranian plateau, and [Armenian plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_plateau) terms to refer to the mountainous area around where [Arabian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_Plate) and [Eurasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_Plate) tectonic plates merge. The eastern Anatolian plateau and Armenian plateau definitions largely overlap.[^cite_note-Oxford_Handbook_p466-200] The [Eastern Anatolia region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Anatolia_region) contains [Mount Ararat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Ararat), Turkey's highest point at 5,137 metres (16,854 feet),[^cite_note-mararat-201] and [Lake Van](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Van), the largest lake in the country.[^cite_note-lvan-202] Eastern Turkey is home to the sources of rivers such as the [Euphrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphrates), [Tigris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tigris) and [Aras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aras_River). The [Southeastern Anatolia Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Anatolia_Region) includes the northern plains of [Upper Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Mesopotamia).&#x20;

[Earthquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_earthquakes_in_Turkey) happen frequently in Turkey.[^cite_note-Ahmed_2006_pp_1575_1576-27] Almost the entire population lives in areas with varying seismic risk levels, with around 70% in highest or second-highest seismic areas.[^cite_note-203][^cite_note-WorldBank_Overview_April_2024-204] [Anatolian plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_plate) is bordered by [North Anatolian Fault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Anatolian_Fault) zone to the north; [East Anatolian Fault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Anatolian_Fault) zone and Bitlis–Zagros collision zone to the east; [Hellenic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_subduction_zone) and Cyprus subduction zones to the south; and [Aegean extensional zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Sea_Plate) to the west.[^cite_note-205] After [1999 İzmit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_%C4%B0zmit_earthquake) and [1999 Düzce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_D%C3%BCzce_earthquake) earthquakes, North Anatolian Fault zone activity "is considered to be one of the most dangerous natural hazards in Turkey".[^cite_note-206] [2023 Turkey–Syria earthquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Turkey%E2%80%93Syria_earthquakes) were the deadliest in contemporary Turkish history.[^cite_note-207] Turkey is sometimes unfavorably compared to [Chile](5489.md), a country with a similar [developmental level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index) that is more successful with [earthquake preparedness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthquake_preparedness).[^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209][^cite_note-210]&#x20;

### Biodiversity

Main articles: [Wildlife of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_Turkey), [Fauna of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna_of_Turkey), and [Flora and vegetation of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flora_and_vegetation_of_Turkey)

See also: [Environmental issues in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_issues_in_Turkey)



[Tulip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulip) is a national flower of Turkey. [Emirgan Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirgan_Park) in Istanbul hosts Tulip Festival each year.

Turkey has rich biodiversity,[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-Demir_Guzel_2024-212][^cite_note-Birben_2019-26] and includes three [biodiversity hotspots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_hotspot)[^cite_note-Demir_Guzel_2024-212][^cite_note-Birben_2019-26] out of the 36 in the world.[^cite_note-Birben_2019-26] These are the [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_Basin), [Irano-Anatolian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irano-Anatolian), and [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus) hotspots.[^cite_note-Birben_2019-26] [Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Anatolian_steppe) or [Eastern Anatolian steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Anatolian_montane_steppe) ecosystems contain wild animal and plant species whose genetic variation have important implications for the food industry, agriculture, and medicine. [Other ecosystems in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ecoregions_in_Turkey) include [Mediterranean plant types](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_forests,_woodlands,_and_scrub), [temperate deciduous forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_deciduous_forest), and [Alpine tundra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_tundra).[^cite_note-MFA_2019-213] An extensive number of species of plants and animals exist in Turkey.[^cite_note-MFA_2019-213] These include around 197 [mammal species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mammals_of_Turkey), 420 [bird species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_birds_of_Turkey), 820 marine and freshwater fish species, 130 [reptile species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_reptiles_of_Turkey), and 10,150 vascular [plant species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flora_of_Turkey).[^cite_note-214] Some of these are [endangered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endangered) or [critically endangered](7451605.md).[^cite_note-MFA_2019-213] A lot of the animal and plant species are [endemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemic).[^cite_note-Birben_2019-26]&#x20;

Landscapes in Turkey face a variety of threats and risks such as [desertification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desertification), [soil erosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_erosion), declining [groundwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groundwater) levels, and [salinization of soil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_salinity). 27% of Turkey is protected, which includes conservation areas, forests, [national parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_parks_of_Turkey), biosphere reserves, [natural monuments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_natural_monuments_of_Turkey), and historical sites.[^cite_note-215] There are 50 national parks;[^cite_note-216] most visited in 2022 were [Marmaris National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marmaris_National_Park) and [Beydağları Coastal National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beyda%C4%9Flar%C4%B1_Coastal_National_Park).[^cite_note-217] As of 2020, [Turkey's forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forests_in_Turkey) covered 29% of Turkey's surface area.[^cite_note-218] Turkey's forests include globally largest concentrations of [Cedrus libani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cedrus_libani) and [Mediterranean cypress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_cypress) trees, while [Turkey's wetlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wetlands_of_Turkey) are significant for [migratory birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migratory_birds).[^cite_note-MFA_2019-213]&#x20;

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Turkey)

See also: [Climate change in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Turkey)





Clockwise from top left image: yearly total precipitation, yearly average temperatures, and Köppen climate types in Turkey

[^cite_note-Taşoğlu_Öztürk_Yazıcı_2024-219]

Turkey's climate is highly varied due to rising overall elevation eastwards and the mountains that run alongside the coasts.[^cite_note-Kuzucuoğlu_2019_p7-192] Warm summers, "cool to cold" winters, and year-round wet conditions are found in the northern coasts. Rainless and hot summers, and wet, cool to mild winters describe the western and southern coasts of the country.[^cite_note-Selek-2018-220] Inland areas feature more severe winters, sharper temperature swings,[^cite_note-Selek-2018-220] and relatively low precipitation.[^cite_note-221]&#x20;

According to the [Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification), Turkey has 14 distinct climate zones.[^cite_note-Taşoğlu_Öztürk_Yazıcı_2024-219] The [Mediterranean climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_climate) is found in the Mediterranean, Aegean, Marmara, and Southeastern Anatolia regions of the country. In the Black Sea area, [humid subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_subtropical) and [oceanic climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate) types are usually found. Large swaths of Central and Eastern Anatolia are classified into [semi-arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-arid) or [continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_climate) types.[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-Turkes_2020_pp105–10-223]&#x20;

Turkey is highly [vulnerable to climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vulnerable_to_climate_change),[^cite_note-World_Bank_climate_change-28][^cite_note-224] due to socioeconomic, climatic, and geographic factors.[^cite_note-World_Bank_climate_change-28] This applies to nine out of ten climate vulnerability dimensions, such as "average annual risk to wellbeing".[^cite_note-World_Bank_climate_change-28] [OECD](33853117.md) median is two out of ten.[^cite_note-World_Bank_climate_change-28] Inclusive and swift growth is needed for decreasing vulnerability.[^cite_note-225] Turkey aims to achieve [net zero emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Net_zero_emissions) by 2053.[^cite_note-World_Bank_Türkiye-226][^cite_note-OECD_2025-2-227] Accomplishing climate goals would require large investments, but would benefit the economy due to less illness and death from [Turkey's air pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_pollution_in_Turkey), and less fuel imports.[^cite_note-228]&#x20;

## Government and politics

Main articles: [Politics of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Turkey) and [Constitution of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Turkey)

The Parliament of Turkey

The [Grand National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_National_Assembly_of_Turkey), \
the [legislative chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_chamber) in [Ankara](802.md)

The Presidential Complex

The [Presidential Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Complex_\(Turkey\)), residence and workplace of the [President of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Turkey)

The Court of Cassation

The [Court of Cassation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Cassation_\(Turkey\)) is Turkey's [supreme court](4913027.md) for reviewing verdicts given by courts of criminal and civil justice.

Turkey is a [presidential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_system) [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic) within a [multi-party system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-party_period_of_the_Republic_of_Turkey).[^cite_note-CIAFactbookTurkey-229] Due to Erdoğan's [judicial capture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_capture), imprisonment of opposition politicians and dissidents, and repression of protest movements, Turkey is not considered a democracy but rather as a [competitive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Competitive_authoritarian) or fully [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian) system.[^cite_note-Borsuk_Levin_2021_pp._175–187-230][^cite_note-231] The [current constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Turkey) was approved by [referendum in 1982](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Turkish_constitutional_referendum), which determines the government's structure, lays forth the ideals and standards of the state's conduct, and sets out the state's responsibility to its citizens. Furthermore, the constitution specifies the people's rights and obligations, as well as principles for the delegation and exercise of sovereignty that belongs to the people of Turkey.[^cite_note-232]&#x20;

In the Turkish [unitary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_system), citizens are subject to [three levels of government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Turkey): national, provincial, and local. The [local government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government)'s duties are commonly split between [municipal governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipal_corporation) and [districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_Turkey), in which the executive and legislative officials are elected by a [plurality vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plurality_vote) of citizens by district. Turkey is subdivided into [81 provinces](428896.md) for administrative purposes. Each province is divided into districts, for a total of [973 districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_districts_in_Turkey).&#x20;

### National government

Main article: [Government of Turkey](3680197.md)

The government, regulated by a system of [separation of powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_powers) as defined by the [constitution of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Turkey), comprises three branches:&#x20;

- [Legislative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative): The [unicameral](204304.md) [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_National_Assembly_of_Turkey) makes [laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law), debates and adopts the budget bills, [declares war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_war), approves treaties, proclaims [amnesty](18940624.md) and [pardon](288593.md), and has the power of [impeachment](15328.md), by which it can remove incumbent members of the government.

  [^cite_note-233]

  &#x20;The Parliament has 600 voting members, each representing a [constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituency) for a five-year term. Parliamentary seats are distributed among the provinces by population, conforming with the census apportionment.

- [Executive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_\(government\)): [The president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Turkey) is the [commander-in-chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander-in-chief) of the military, can veto [legislative bills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_\(law\)) before they become law (subject to parliamentary override), can issue [presidential decrees](322949.md) on matters regarding executive power with the exception of fundamental rights, individual rights and certain political rights (parliamentary laws prevail presidential decrees), and appoints the [members of the Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Turkey) and other officers, who administer and enforce national laws and policies.

  [^cite_note-234]

  &#x20;The president is [elected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_presidential_elections) by [direct vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_vote) and serves a five-year term. The president cannot run for re-elections after two terms of five-years, unless the parliament prematurely renews the presidential elections during the second term of the President. Elections for the Parliament and presidential elections are held on the same day.

- [Judicial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial): The [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Turkey) (for [constitutional adjudication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutionality) and review of individual applications concerning human rights), the [Court of Cassation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Cassation_of_Turkey) (final decision maker in ordinary judiciary), the [Council of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Council_of_State) (final decision maker in administrative judiciary) and the [Court of Jurisdictional Disputes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Jurisdictional_Disputes) (for resolving the disputes between courts for constitutional jurisdiction) are the four organizations that are described by the Constitution as supreme courts. The Constitutional Court is composed of fifteen judges and are appointed by the president and the parliament.

  [^cite_note-Constitution2019-5]

  &#x20;A member is elected for a term of twelve years and cannot be re-elected. The members of the Constitutional Court are obliged to retire when they are over the age of sixty-five.

  [^cite_note-235]

The three-branch system is known as the [presidential system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_system), in contrast to the [parliamentary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system) where the executive is part of the legislative body.&#x20;

### Administrative divisions

Main article: [Administrative divisions of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Turkey)

Further information: [Regions of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Turkey) and [NUTS of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NUTS_of_Turkey)

Turkey is a [unitary state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state). Its administrative system includes central and local administration. Central administration consists of the [central government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_government) in [Ankara](802.md), and local departments such as 81 [provinces](428896.md) and [their subdivisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_Turkey). Local administration authorities consist of metropolitan municipalities, municipalities, neighborhoods or villages, and special provincial administrations. For economic and geographic reasons, Turkey is also categorized into [seven regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographical_regions_of_Turkey) and 21 sub-regions.[^cite_note-236]&#x20;
