### Ethnicity and language

See also: [Turkish people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_people), [Minorities in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minorities_in_Turkey), [Languages of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Turkey), and [Turkish language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language)



Percentage of [ethnic Kurds in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurds_in_Turkey) by region (2010)

[^cite_note-KONDAkurd-391]

Article 66 of the [Turkish Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Constitution) defines a *Turk* as anyone who is a citizen.[^cite_note-392] It is estimated that there are at least 47 ethnic groups represented in Turkey.[^cite_note-mutlu-393] Reliable data on the ethnic mix of the population is not available because census figures do not include statistics on ethnicity after the [1965 Turkish census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1965_Turkish_census).[^cite_note-Turkey_Ethnic_groups-394] In 2021, 77% of adult citizens identified as *Turk*.[^cite_note-Konda_100-395]&#x20;

[Kurds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurds_in_Turkey) are the largest ethnic minority.[^cite_note-Kirişci_1997_119–121-396] Their exact numbers remain disputed,[^cite_note-Kirişci_1997_119–121-396] with estimates ranging from 12 to 20% of the population.[^cite_note-397] The Kurds make up a majority in the provinces of [Ağrı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A%C4%9Fr%C4%B1_Province), [Batman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batman_Province), [Bingöl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bing%C3%B6l_Province), [Bitlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitlis_Province), [Diyarbakır](793511.md), [Hakkari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakkari_Province), [Iğdır](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I%C4%9Fd%C4%B1r_Province), [Mardin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mardin_Province), [Muş](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mu%C5%9F_Province), [Siirt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siirt_Province), [Şırnak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9E%C4%B1rnak_Province), [Tunceli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunceli_Province) and [Van](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Van_Province); a near majority in [Şanlıurfa](1353138.md) (47%); and a large minority in [Kars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kars_Province) (20%).[^cite_note-398] In addition, internal migration has resulted in [Kurdish diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_diaspora) communities in all of the major cities in central and western Turkey. In Istanbul, there are an estimated three million Kurds, making it the city with the largest Kurdish population in the world.[^cite_note-Nachmani2003-399] 19% of adult citizens identified as ethnic Kurds in a survey in 2021.[^cite_note-Konda_100-395] Some people have multiple ethnic identities, such as both Turk and Kurd.[^cite_note-400][^cite_note-401] In 2006, an estimated 2.7 million ethnic Turks and Kurds were related from interethnic marriages.[^cite_note-402]&#x20;



The distribution of the [Turkic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_languages)

According to the World Factbook, non-Kurdish ethnic minorities are 7–12% of the population.[^cite_note-cia-4] In 2006, KONDA estimated that non-Kurdish and non-Zaza ethnic minorities constituted 8.2% of the population; these were people who gave general descriptions such as Turkish citizen, people with [other Turkic backgrounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_people), [Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Turkey), and others.[^cite_note-KONDA_2006_17-403] In 2021, 4% of adult citizens identified as non-ethnic Turk or non-ethnic Kurd in a survey.[^cite_note-Konda_100-395] According to the Constitutional Court, there are only four officially recognized [minorities in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minorities_in_Turkey): the three non-Muslim minorities recognized in the [Treaty of Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Lausanne) ([Armenians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenians_in_Turkey), [Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Turkey), and [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_in_Turkey)[^cite_note-405]) and the [Bulgarians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarians_in_Turkey).[^cite_note-408][^cite_note-Toktaş2009-409][^cite_note-Yağmur2001-410][^cite_note-Zetler2014-411] In 2013, the Ankara 13th Circuit Administrative Court ruled that the minority provisions of the Lausanne Treaty should also apply to [Assyrians in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrians_in_Turkey) and the [Syriac language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_language).[^cite_note-412][^cite_note-Akbulut2023-413][^cite_note-Erdem2021-414] Other unrecognized ethnic groups include [Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Tatars_in_Turkey), [Albanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanians_in_Turkey), [Bosniaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosniaks_in_Turkey), [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians_in_Turkey), [Georgians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgians_in_Turkey), [Laz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laz_people), [Pomaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomaks), and [Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people).[^cite_note-Roopnarine2015-415][^cite_note-416][^cite_note-417]&#x20;

The [official language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language) is [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language), which is the most widely spoken [Turkic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_language) in the world.[^cite_note-katzner-418][^cite_note-minorityrights.org-419] It is spoken by 85%[^cite_note-KONDA_2006_19-420][^cite_note-milliyet-languages-421] to 90%[^cite_note-Kornfilt_2018_537-422] of the population as a [first language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_language). Kurdish speakers are the largest linguistic minority.[^cite_note-Kornfilt_2018_537-422] A survey estimated 13% of the population speak Kurdish or Zaza as a first language.[^cite_note-KONDA_2006_19-420] Other minority languages include Arabic, [Caucasian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasian_languages), and [Gagauz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gagauz_language).[^cite_note-Kornfilt_2018_537-422] The [linguistic rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linguistic_rights) of the officially recognized minorities are *de jure* recognized and protected for [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_language), [Bulgarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_language), [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language), [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew),[^cite_note-423][^cite_note-Bayır2013-404][^cite_note-Toktaş2009-409][^cite_note-Yağmur2001-410][^cite_note-Zetler2014-411] and [Syriac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_language).[^cite_note-Akbulut2023-413][^cite_note-Erdem2021-414] There are multiple [endangered languages in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_endangered_languages_in_Asia#Turkey).&#x20;

### Immigration

Main article: [Immigration to Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Turkey)

Excluding Syrians under temporary protection, there were 1,570,543 foreign citizens in Turkey in 2023.[^cite_note-Population_of_Turkey-7]&#x20;

Following the suppression of the Kurdish uprising in spring 1991, after the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) ceasefire, hundreds of thousands of Kurds fled toward the Turkish border. Turkey largely refused entry, leaving many stranded in the mountains; the UNHCR estimated around 280,000 had crossed into Turkey by April 6, 1991.[^cite_note-424] Most returned to Iraq within months after the establishment of a UN-protected safe haven in northern Iraq, with only around 8,000 remaining in Turkey by early 1992.[^cite_note-425]&#x20;

[Turkey's migrant crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey%27s_migrant_crisis) in the 2010s and early 2020s resulted in the influx of millions of refugees and immigrants.[^cite_note-esa.un.org-426] Turkey hosts the largest number of refugees in the world as of April 2020.[^cite_note-unhcr-syrian-refugees-427] The [Disaster and Emergency Management Presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disaster_and_Emergency_Management_Presidency) manages the refugee crisis in Turkey. Before the start of the [Syrian civil war](30741795.md) in 2011, the estimated number of [Arabs in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Turkey) varied from 1 million to more than 2 million.[^cite_note-428]&#x20;

In November 2020, there were 3.6 million [Syrian refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_of_the_Syrian_civil_war_in_Turkey) in Turkey;[^cite_note-429] these included other [ethnic groups of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_of_Syria), such as [Syrian Kurds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Kurds)[^cite_note-430] and [Syrian Turkmens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Turkmen).[^cite_note-431] As of August 2023, the number of these refugees was estimated to be 3.3 million. The number of Syrians had decreased by about 200,000 people since the beginning of the year.[^cite_note-432] The government has granted citizenship to 238 thousand Syrians by November 2023.[^cite_note-433] As of May 2023, approximately 96,000 Ukrainian [refugees of the 2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_of_the_2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) have sought refuge in Turkey.[^cite_note-434] In 2022, nearly 100,000 Russian citizens migrated to Turkey, becoming the first in the list of foreigners who moved to Turkey, meaning an increase of more than 218% from 2021.[^cite_note-435]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Turkey)



[Sultan Ahmed Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan_Ahmed_Mosque), also known as Blue Mosque in Istanbul, is one of the most iconic and popular monuments of [Ottoman architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_architecture).

Turkey is a [secular state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_state) with no official [state religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion); the constitution provides for [freedom of religion](46006.md) and [conscience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_conscience).[^cite_note-436][^cite_note-437] According to the [CIA World Factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA_World_Factbook), Muslims constitute 99.8% of the population, most of them being [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni).[^cite_note-cia-4] Based on a survey, [KONDA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KONDA_Research_and_Consultancy)'s estimate for Muslims was 99.4% in 2006.[^cite_note-KONDA_2006-438] According to [Minority Rights Group International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_Rights_Group_International), estimates of share of [Alevi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alevi) are between 10% and 40% of the population.[^cite_note-refworld.org1-439] KONDA's estimate was 5% in 2006.[^cite_note-KONDA_2006-438] 4% of adult citizens identified as Alevi in a survey in 2021, while 88% identified as Sunni.[^cite_note-Konda_100-395]&#x20;

The percentage of non-Muslims in modern-day Turkey was 19.1% in 1914, but fell to 2.5% in 1927.[^cite_note-440] Currently, non-Muslims constitute 0.2% of the population according to the World Factbook.[^cite_note-cia-4] In 2006, KONDA's estimate was that 0.18% of the population adhered to non-Islamic religions.[^cite_note-KONDA_2006-438] Some of the non-Muslim communities are Armenians, [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrians_in_Turkey), Bulgarian Orthodox, [Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Turkey), [Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy_in_Turkey), Jews, and [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_Turkey).[^cite_note-441] Sources estimate that the [Christian population in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Turkey) ranges between 180,000 and 320,000.[^cite_note-442][^cite_note-443] Turkey has [the largest Jewish community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_population_by_country) among the Muslim-majority countries.[^cite_note-444] Currently, there are 439 churches and synagogues in Turkey.[^cite_note-445]&#x20;

In 2006, KONDA estimated that 0.47% of the population had no religion.[^cite_note-KONDA_2006-438] According to KONDA, the share of adult citizens who identified as nonbelievers increased from 2% in 2011 to 6% in 2021.[^cite_note-Konda_100-395] A 2020 Gezici Araştırma poll found that 28.5% of the [Generation Z](4015968.md) [identified as irreligious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_Turkey).[^cite_note-446][^cite_note-447]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Turkey)



Enrollment rates for [primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_education), [secondary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_education) and [tertiary education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_education) in Turkey between 1971 and 2022. Completion rates for primary and lower secondary education.

[^cite_note-448]

In the past 20 years, Turkey has improved quality of education and has made significant progress in increasing education access.[^cite_note-OECD_Taking_stock_p3-449] From 2011 to 2021, improvements in education access include significant rise in the rates of [upper secondary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_secondary) and [tertiary education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_education) completion, and quadrupling of pre-school institutions.[^cite_note-OECDp35-30] [PISA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment) results suggest improvements in education quality.[^cite_note-OECDp35-30] There is still a gap with OECD countries. Significant challenges include differences in student outcomes from different schools, differences between rural and urban areas, pre-primary education access, and arrival of students who are Syrian refugees.[^cite_note-OECDp35-30]&#x20;

The [Ministry of National Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_National_Education_\(Turkey\)) is responsible for pre-tertiary education.[^cite_note-wesed-450] Compulsory education is free at public schools and lasts 12 years, divided into three parts.[^cite_note-451][^cite_note-OECD_Taking_stock_p3-449] There are 208 [universities in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universities_in_Turkey).[^cite_note-euraxess_Türkiye-382] Students are placed to universities based on their [YKS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_Selection_and_Placement_System) results and their preferences, by the [Measuring, Selection and Placement Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Measuring,_Selection_and_Placement_Center).[^cite_note-452] All state and private universities are under the control of the [Higher Education Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Higher_Education_\(Turkey\)). Since 2016, the president of Turkey directly appoints all rectors of all state and private universities.[^cite_note-453]&#x20;

According to the 2024 [Times Higher Education ranking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Times_Higher_Education_World_University_Rankings), the top universities were [Koç University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ko%C3%A7_University), [Middle East Technical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East_Technical_University), [Sabancı University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabanc%C4%B1_University), and [Istanbul Technical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Technical_University).[^cite_note-454] According to [Academic Ranking of World Universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_Ranking_of_World_Universities), the top ones were [Istanbul University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_University), [University of Health Sciences (Turkey)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Health_Sciences_\(Turkey\)), and [Hacettepe University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hacettepe_University).[^cite_note-455] For foreign students, Turkey has become a regional hub,[^cite_note-456] with a large number of international students[^cite_note-457] and [international scholarships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%BCrkiye_Scholarships).[^cite_note-458][^cite_note-459][^cite_note-460]&#x20;

### Health

Main article: [Health in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Turkey)

See also: [Health care in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_in_Turkey)



[Başakşehir Çam and Sakura City Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba%C5%9Fak%C5%9Fehir_%C3%87am_and_Sakura_City_Hospital) in Istanbul. It contains 2,068 [seismic base isolation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seismic_base_isolation) units to [withstand earthquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthquake-resistant_structures).

[^cite_note-461]

The [Ministry of Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Health_\(Turkey\)) has run a universal public healthcare system since 2003.[^cite_note-njm-29] Known as Universal Health Insurance (*Genel Sağlık Sigortası*), it is funded by a tax surcharge on employers, currently at 5%.[^cite_note-njm-29] Public-sector funding covers approximately 75.2% of health expenditures.[^cite_note-njm-29] Despite the universal health care, total expenditure on health as a share of GDP in 2018 was the lowest among OECD countries at 6.3% of GDP, compared to the OECD average of 9.3%.[^cite_note-njm-29] There are many private hospitals in the country.[^cite_note-Oguz_2020-462] The government planned several hospital complexes, known as city hospitals, to be constructed since 2013.[^cite_note-Oguz_2020-462] Turkey is one of the top 10 destinations for [health tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_tourism#Turkey).[^cite_note-463]&#x20;

Average life expectancy is 78.6 years (75.9 for males and 81.3 for females), compared with the EU average of 81 years.[^cite_note-njm-29] Turkey has high rates of [obesity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obesity), with 29.5% of its adult population having a [body mass index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body_mass_index) (BMI) value of 30 or above.[^cite_note-WHO_Mean_Body_Mass_Index_BMI-464] [Air pollution is a major cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_pollution_in_Turkey) of early death.[^cite_note-465]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Turkey)

See also: [Arts in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arts_in_Turkey), [Turkish folklore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_folklore), and [Festivals in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festivals_in_Turkey)

In the 19th century, Turkish identity was debated in the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), with three main views: Turkism, Islamism and Westernism.[^cite_note-466] In addition to Europe or Islam, Turkish culture was also influenced by Anatolia's native cultures.[^cite_note-467] After the establishment of the republic, [Kemalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemalism) emphasized Turkish culture, attempted to make "Islam a matter of personal conviction", and pursued modernization.[^cite_note-468] Currently, Turkey has various local cultures. Things such as music, [folk dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_folk_dance), or [kebap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kebap) variety may be used to identify a local area. Turkey also has a national culture, such as national sports leagues, music bands, film stars, and trends in fashion.[^cite_note-469] Turkey is home to [21 UNESCO World Heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Turkey) sites and [31 UNESCO intangible cultural heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intangible_cultural_heritage) inscriptions.[^cite_note-470]&#x20;

### Architecture

Main article: [Architecture of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Turkey)

See also: [Ottoman architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_architecture)



Built by [Mimar Sinan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimar_Sinan), [Selimiye Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selimiye_Mosque,_Edirne) in [Edirne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edirne) is an example of [classical Ottoman architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Ottoman_architecture).



[Tayyare Apartments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tayyare_Apartments) in [Istanbul](3391396.md), an example of the [first national architectural movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_national_architectural_movement)



With its traditional Turkish houses, [Odunpazarı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odunpazar%C4%B1) district in [Eskişehir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eski%C5%9Fehir) is a [tentative UNESCO World Heritage site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tentative_list_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Turkey).

[^cite_note-471]

Turkey is home to numerous [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) settlements, such as [Çatalhöyük](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%87atalh%C3%B6y%C3%BCk).[^cite_note-472][^cite_note-Howard_2016-3-56] From the [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age), important architectural remnants include [Alaca Höyük](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaca_H%C3%B6y%C3%BCk) and the [2nd layer of Troy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troy#Troy_II).[^cite_note-Howard_2016-3-56] There are various examples of [Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_architecture) and [Ancient Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Roman_architecture) architectures, especially in the Aegean region.[^cite_note-473] [Byzantine architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_architecture) dates back to the 4th century AD. Its best example is [Hagia Sophia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia). Byzantine architectural style continued to develop after the [conquest of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquest_of_Istanbul), such as [Byzantine Revival architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Revival_architecture).[^cite_note-474] During [Seljuk Sultanate of Rum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seljuk_Sultanate_of_Rum) and [Turkish principalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_beyliks) period, a distinct architecture emerged, which incorporated Byzantine and [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_architecture) architectures with architectural styles found in [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia) and [Central Asia](6742.md).[^cite_note-475] [Seljuk architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_Seljuk_architecture) often used stones and bricks, and produced numerous [caravanserais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caravanserai), [madrasas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrasa) and [mausoleums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mausoleum).[^cite_note-476]&#x20;

[Ottoman architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_architecture) emerged in northwest Anatolia and Thrace. [Early Ottoman architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Ottoman_architecture) mixed "traditional Anatolian Islamic architecture with local building materials and techniques".[^cite_note-477] Following the conquest of Istanbul, [classical Ottoman architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Ottoman_architecture) emerged in the 16th and 17th centuries.[^cite_note-Bloom-2-478] The most important architect of the classical period is [Mimar Sinan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimar_Sinan), whose major works include the [Şehzade Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Eehzade_Mosque), [Süleymaniye Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%BCleymaniye_Mosque), and [Selimiye Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selimiye_Mosque,_Edirne).[^cite_note-Bloom-2-478] Beginning in the 18th century, Ottoman architecture was influenced by European elements, resulting in development of [Ottoman baroque style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Baroque_architecture).[^cite_note-Bloom-479] European influence continued in the 19th century; examples include works of [Balyan family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balyan_family) such as [neo-Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_Revival_architecture) style [Dolmabahçe Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolmabah%C3%A7e_Palace).[^cite_note-480] The last period of Ottoman architecture consists of the [First National Architectural Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_National_Architectural_Movement), including works of [Vedat Tek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedat_Tek) and [Mimar Kemaleddin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimar_Kemaleddin).[^cite_note-Bloom-479]&#x20;

Turkish architecture since 1918 can be divided into three periods. 1918 to 1950 includes the First National Architectural Movement, which transitioned into [modernist architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernist_architecture). Modernist and monumental buildings were preferred for public buildings, whereas "Turkish house" type [vernacular architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vernacular_architecture) influenced private houses. The period from 1950 to 1980 includes urbanization, modernization, and internationalization. For residential housing, "reinforced concrete, slab-block, medium-rise apartments" became prevalent. Since 1980, Turkish architecture has been defined by consumer habits and international trends, such as shopping malls and office towers. Luxury residences with "Turkish house style" have been in demand.[^cite_note-481] In the 21st century, [urban renewal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_renewal) projects have become a trend.[^cite_note-482] Resilience against natural disasters such as earthquakes is one of the main goals for urban renewal projects.[^cite_note-483] Around one-third of Turkey's building stock, 6.7 million units, have been assessed as risky and needing urban renewal.[^cite_note-484]&#x20;

### Literature, theatre, and visual arts

Main articles: [Turkish literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_literature), [Theatre of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_Turkey), and [Turkish art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_art)



[Nobel](23385442.md)-laureate Turkish novelist [Orhan Pamuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orhan_Pamuk) and his [Turkish Angora cat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Angora_cat) at his personal writing space



[Süreyya Opera House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%BCreyya_Opera_House) is on the Asian side of [Istanbul](3391396.md) and [Atatürk Cultural Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atat%C3%BCrk_Cultural_Center) is the main [opera house](155040.md) on the European side. [Zorlu PSM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zorlu_PSM) is the city's largest [performing arts theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Performing_arts) and [concert hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concert_hall).

[Turkish literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_literature) goes back more than a thousand years. The Seljuk and Ottoman periods include numerous works of literature and poetry. Turkic tales and poetry from Central Asia were also kept alive. [Tales of Dede Korkut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Dede_Korkut) is an example of the [oral narrative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_storytelling) tradition. [Dīwān Lughāt al-Turk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C4%ABw%C4%81n_Lugh%C4%81t_al-Turk), from the 11th century, contains Turkish linguistic information and poetry. [Yunus Emre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yunus_Emre), influenced by [Rumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumi), was one of the most important writers of Anatolian Turkish poetry. Ottoman [Divan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diwan_\(poetry\)) poetry used "refined diction" and complex vocabulary. It included [Sufi mysticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufi_mysticism), romanticism, and formal elements.[^cite_note-Halman_2012_Literature-485]&#x20;

From the 19th century, Ottoman literature was influenced by the West. New genres, such as novels and journalistic style, were introduced. *[Aşk-ı Memnu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A%C5%9Fk-%C4%B1_Memnu_\(novel\))*, written by [Halid Ziya Uşaklıgil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halid_Ziya_U%C5%9Fakl%C4%B1gil), was the "first truly refined Turkish novel". [Fatma Aliye Topuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatma_Aliye_Topuz), the first female Turkish novelist, wrote fiction. After the proclamation of the republic in 1923, [Atatürk instituted reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atat%C3%BCrk%27s_reforms) such as the [language reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language_reform) and [alphabet reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_alphabet_reform). Since then, Turkish literature reflected the socioeconomic conditions in Turkey with increasing variety. "Village Novel" genre appeared in the mid-1950s, which talked about difficulties faced from poverty.[^cite_note-Halman_2012_Literature-485] An example is [Memed, My Hawk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memed,_My_Hawk) by [Yaşar Kemal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ya%C5%9Far_Kemal), which was [Turkey's first Nobel Prize in Literature nominee in 1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Nobel_Prize_in_Literature).[^cite_note-Halman_2012_Literature-485][^cite_note-486] [Orhan Pamuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orhan_Pamuk) won the [2006 Nobel Prize in Literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Nobel_Prize_in_Literature).[^cite_note-Halman_2012_Literature-485]&#x20;

Turkey has four "major theatrical traditions": "folk theatre, popular theatre, court theater, and Western theater." Turkish folk theatre goes back thousands of years and has survived among rural communities. Popular theatre includes plays by live actors, puppet and [shadow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_play) plays, and [storytelling performances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meddah). An example for shadow play is *[Karagöz and Hacivat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karag%C3%B6z_and_Hacivat)*. Court theatre was the refined version of popular theatre. Beginning in the 19th century, Western theatre tradition started appearing in Turkey. Following the establishment of Turkish Republic, a state conservatory and the State Theatre Company were formed.[^cite_note-487]&#x20;

Turkey's visual arts scene can be categorized into two, as "decorative" and "fine" arts. Fine arts, or *güzel sanatlar*, includes sculpture and [painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Modern_Turkish_painting). Turkish artists in these areas have gained global recognition. Photography, fashion design, graphic arts, and graphic design are some of the other areas Turkish artists are known for in the world. The inaugural contemporary Turkish art sale by [Sotheby's London](541267.md) was in 2009. [Istanbul Modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Modern) and the [Istanbul Biennial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Biennial) are examples of art galleries or exhibitions of contemporary Turkish art. Turkey has also seen a resurgence of traditional arts. This includes Ottoman-era traditional arts, such as [ceramics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iznik_pottery) and [carpets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_rug). Textile and carpet design, glass and ceramics, [calligraphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_calligraphy), [paper marbling (ebru)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paper_marbling) are some of the art forms for which modern-day Turkish artists are recognized as leaders in the Islamic world.[^cite_note-Denny_2012_Fine_Arts-488]&#x20;

### Music and dance

Main articles: [Music of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Turkey) and [Turkish folk dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_folk_dance)



[Barış Manço](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar%C4%B1%C5%9F_Man%C3%A7o) was a Turkish rock musician and one of the founders of the [Anatolian rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_rock) genre.

Although classifying genres of Turkish music can be problematic, three broad categories can be considered. These are "[Turkish folk music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_folk_music)", "[Turkish art music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_art_music)", and multiple popular music styles. These Popular music styles include [arabesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabesque_\(Turkish_music\)), pop, and [Anatolian rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_rock).[^cite_note-489]&#x20;

The resurging popularity of pop music gave rise to several international Turkish pop stars such as [Ajda Pekkan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajda_Pekkan), [Sezen Aksu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sezen_Aksu), [Erol Evgin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erol_Evgin), [MFÖ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MF%C3%96), [Tarkan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarkan_\(singer\)), [Mustafa Sandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Sandal), [Sertab Erener](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sertab_Erener), [Teoman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teoman_\(singer\)), [Kenan Doğulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenan_Do%C4%9Fulu), [Levent Yüksel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levent_Y%C3%BCksel) and [Hande Yener](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hande_Yener). Internationally acclaimed Turkish [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) and [blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blues) musicians and composers include [Ahmet Ertegun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmet_Ertegun).[^cite_note-490]&#x20;

### Media and cinema

Main articles: [Media in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_in_Turkey) and [Cinema of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Turkey)

Hundreds of television channels, thousands of local and national radio stations, several dozen newspapers, a productive and profitable [national cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Turkey) and a rapid growth of [broadband](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadband) Internet use constitute a vibrant media industry in Turkey.[^cite_note-tesevmedya-491][^cite_note-492] The majority of the TV audiences are shared among public broadcaster [TRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TRT) and the network-style channels such as [Kanal D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanal_D), [Show TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Show_TV), [ATV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ATV_\(Turkey\)) and [Star TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_TV_\(Turkey\)). The [broadcast media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadcast_media) have a very high penetration as [satellite dishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_dishes) and [cable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cable_television) systems are widely available.[^cite_note-cp-493] The [Radio and Television Supreme Council (RTÜK)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RT%C3%9CK) is the government body overseeing the broadcast media.[^cite_note-494] By circulation, the [most popular newspapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_Turkey) are *[Posta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Posta_\(newspaper\))*, *[Hürriyet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%BCrriyet)*, *[Sözcü](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%B6zc%C3%BC)*, *[Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_\(newspaper\))* and *[Habertürk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habert%C3%BCrk)*.[^cite_note-495]&#x20;

[Filiz Akın](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filiz_Ak%C4%B1n), [Fatma Girik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatma_Girik), [Hülya Koçyiğit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%BClya_Ko%C3%A7yi%C4%9Fit), and [Türkan Şoray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%BCrkan_%C5%9Eoray) represent their period of Turkish cinema.[^cite_note-Magnan-496] Turkish directors like [Metin Erksan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metin_Erksan), [Nuri Bilge Ceylan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuri_Bilge_Ceylan), [Yılmaz Güney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y%C4%B1lmaz_G%C3%BCney), [Zeki Demirkubuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeki_Demirkubuz) and [Ferzan Özpetek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferzan_%C3%96zpetek) won numerous international awards such as the [Palme d'Or](612946.md) and [Golden Bear](21547166.md).[^cite_note-berlinale_1964-497] [Turkish television dramas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_television_drama) are increasingly becoming popular beyond Turkey's borders and are among the country's most vital exports, both in terms of profit and public relations.[^cite_note-498] After sweeping the [Middle East](19323.md)'s television market over the past decade, Turkish shows have aired in more than a dozen [South](26769.md) and [Central American](6121.md) countries in 2016.[^cite_note-499] Turkey is today the world's second largest exporter of television series.[^cite_note-500][^cite_note-501][^cite_note-502]&#x20;

The [World Press Freedom Index](8264708.md) 2026 found serious [censorship in Turkey](9790999.md).[^cite_note-\:3-503] According to the [Committee to Protect Journalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_to_Protect_Journalists), there are 13 [jailed journalists in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_arrested_journalists_in_Turkey).[^cite_note-504]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Turkish cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_cuisine)

See also: [Ottoman cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_cuisine)



[Turkish coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_coffee) with [Turkish delight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_delight). Turkish coffee is a [UNESCO-listed intangible cultural heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO_Intangible_Cultural_Heritage_Lists) of [Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_people).

[^cite_note-505]

[^cite_note-506]

Turkey has a diverse and rich cuisine, varying geographically.[^cite_note-Yayla_Aktaş_2021-507] Turkish cuisine has been influenced by Anatolian, [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_cuisine), [Iranian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_cuisine), [Central Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Asian_cuisine), and [East Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_cuisine#East_Asian_cuisine) cuisines.[^cite_note-Oxford_Companion_to_Food_loc_Turkey-508] Turkish and Ottoman cuisine have also influenced others. [Dīwān Lughāt al-Turk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C4%ABw%C4%81n_Lugh%C4%81t_al-Turk), from the 11th century, documents "the ancient lineage of much of present-day Turkish cuisine".[^cite_note-Oxford_Companion_to_Food_loc_Turkey-508] [Güveç](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%BCve%C3%A7), [Bulgur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgur), and [Börek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%B6rek) are some of the earliest recorded examples of Turkish cuisine. Even though [kebab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kebab) as a word comes from [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language), Turkic people had been familiar with using skewers to cook meat. Turkish cuisine can be distinguished by its various kinds of kebabs. Similarly, [pilaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pilaf) dishes were influenced by Turkish cuisine. Further information about cuisine during the Seljuk and Ottoman periods comes from the works of [Rumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumi) and [Evliya Çelebi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evliya_%C3%87elebi). The latter describes "food-related guilds of Istanbul".[^cite_note-Oxford_Companion_to_Food_loc_Turkey-508]&#x20;

Food staples in Turkey include [bread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bread) and [yogurt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogurt). Some of bread varieties are [lavash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lavash) and *pide* (a type of [pita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pita) bread). [Ayran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayran) is a drink made of yoghurt. In western parts of Turkey, [olive oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive_oil) is used. Grains include wheat, maize, barley, oats, and millet. Beans, chickpeas, nuts, aubergines, and lamb are some of the commonly used ingredients.[^cite_note-Oxford_Companion_to_Food_loc_Turkey-508] [Doner kebab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doner_kebab), originally from Turkey, is marinated lamb slices cooked vertically.[^cite_note-509] Seafood includes [anchovy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchovy) and others. [Dolma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolma) varieties and *[mantı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mant%C4%B1)* are made by stuffing vegetables or pasta.[^cite_note-Oxford_Companion_to_Food_loc_Turkey-508] [Sarma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarma_\(food\)) is made by rolling edible leaf over the filling.[^cite_note-510] [Yahni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahni) dishes are vegetable stews.[^cite_note-Oxford_Companion_to_Food_loc_Turkey-508] Turkey is one of the countries with the [meze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meze) tradition.[^cite_note-511] Honey, [pekmez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pekmez), dried fruit, or fruit are used for sweetening.[^cite_note-Oxford_Companion_to_Food_loc_Turkey-508] [Filo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filo) is an originally Turkish [dough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dough) that is used to make [baklava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baklava).[^cite_note-512] [Turkish delight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_delight) is a "delicate but gummy jelly".[^cite_note-513]&#x20;

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Turkey)

See also: [Football in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_Turkey)

The most popular sport is [association football](10568.md). In addition, [basketball](3921.md), [volleyball](32558.md) and [tennis](29773.md) have gained media coverage recently.[^cite_note-514] [Galatasaray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galatasaray_S.K._\(football\)) won the [UEFA Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_UEFA_Cup_Final) and [UEFA Super Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_UEFA_Super_Cup) in 2000.[^cite_note-uefa.com-515] The [Turkey national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey_national_football_team) won the bronze medal at the [2002 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_FIFA_World_Cup), the [2003 FIFA Confederations Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_FIFA_Confederations_Cup) and [UEFA Euro 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2008).[^cite_note-tff.org1-516]&#x20;

Other mainstream sports such as basketball and volleyball are also popular.[^cite_note-517] The [men's national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey_men%27s_national_basketball_team) and [women's national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey_women%27s_national_basketball_team) have been successful. [Anadolu Efes S.K.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anadolu_Efes_S.K.) is the most successful Turkish basketball club in international competitions.[^cite_note-518][^cite_note-519] [Fenerbahçe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fenerbah%C3%A7e_Men%27s_Basketball) reached the final of the [EuroLeague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroLeague) in three consecutive seasons ([2015–2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015%E2%80%9316_Euroleague), [2016–2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016%E2%80%9317_EuroLeague) and [2017–2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017%E2%80%9318_EuroLeague)), becoming the European champions in 2017.&#x20;



[VakıfBank S.K.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vak%C4%B1fBank_S.K.) is one of the best women's volleyball team in the world, having won the [FIVB World Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIVB_Volleyball_Women%27s_Club_World_Championship) four times and the [CEV Champions Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CEV_Women%27s_Champions_League) six times.

The final of the [2013–14 EuroLeague Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013%E2%80%9314_EuroLeague_Women) basketball championship was played between two Turkish teams, [Galatasaray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galatasaray_S.K._\(women%27s_basketball\)) and [Fenerbahçe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fenerbah%C3%A7e_Women%27s_Basketball), and won by Galatasaray.[^cite_note-fibaeurope.com2-520] Fenerbahçe won the [2023 FIBA Europe SuperCup Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_FIBA_Europe_SuperCup_Women) after two consecutive Euroleague wins in the [2022–23](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%9323_EuroLeague_Women) and [2023–24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023%E2%80%9324_EuroLeague_Women) seasons.&#x20;

The [women's national volleyball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey_women%27s_national_volleyball_team) has won several medals.[^cite_note-tvf.org.tr-521] Women's volleyball clubs, namely [VakıfBank S.K.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vak%C4%B1fBank_S.K.), [Fenerbahçe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fenerbah%C3%A7e_Women%27s_Volleyball) and [Eczacıbaşı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eczac%C4%B1ba%C5%9F%C4%B1_Dynavit), have won numerous European championship titles and medals.[^cite_note-CEV-2023-522]&#x20;

The traditional national sport of Turkey has been [yağlı güreş](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ya%C4%9Fl%C4%B1_g%C3%BCre%C5%9F) (*[oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive_oil) [wrestling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrestling)*) since Ottoman times.[^cite_note-523] Edirne Province has hosted the annual [Kırkpınar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%B1rkp%C4%B1nar) oil wrestling tournament since 1361, making it the oldest continuously held sporting competition in the world.[^cite_note-524][^cite_note-525] In the 19th and early 20th centuries, oil wrestling champions such as [Koca Yusuf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koca_Yusuf), [Nurullah Hasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nurullah_Hasan) and [Kızılcıklı Mahmut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%B1z%C4%B1lc%C4%B1kl%C4%B1_Mahmut) acquired international fame in Europe and North America by winning world heavyweight wrestling championship titles. International wrestling styles governed by [FILA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Federation_of_Associated_Wrestling_Styles) such as [freestyle wrestling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freestyle_wrestling) and [Greco-Roman wrestling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Roman_wrestling) are also popular, with many European, World and Olympic championship titles won by Turkish wrestlers both individually and as a national team.[^cite_note-526]&#x20;

[^cite_note-15]: &#x20;[Ahmed 2006](#CITEREFAhmed2006), p. 1576: "Turkey's diversity is derived from its central location near the world's earliest civilizations as well as a history replete with population movements and invasions. The Hattite culture was prominent during the Bronze Age prior to 2000 BCE, but was replaced by the Indo-European Hittites who conquered Anatolia by the second millennium. Meanwhile, Turkish Thrace came to be dominated by another Indo-European group, the Thracians for whom the region is named."[Steadman 2012](#CITEREFSteadman2012), p. 234: "By the time of the Old Assyrian Colony period in the early second millennium b.c.e . (see Michel, chapter 13 in this volume) the languages spoken on the plateau included Hattian, an indigenous Anatolian language, Hurrian (spoken in northern Syria), and Indo-European languages known as Luwian, Hittite, and Palaic"[Michel 2012](#CITEREFMichel2012), p. 327[Melchert 2012](#CITEREFMelchert2012), p. 713[Howard 2016](#CITEREFHoward2016), p. 26

[^cite_note-16]: &#x20;[Howard 2016](#CITEREFHoward2016), p. 29: "The sudden disappearance of the Persian Empire and the conquest of virtually the entire Middle Eastern world from the Nile to the Indus by Alexander the Great caused tremendous political and cultural upheaval. \[...] statesmen throughout the conquered regions attempted to implement a policy of Hellenization. For indigenous elites, this amounted to the forced assimilation of native religion and culture to Greek models. It met resistance in Anatolia as elsewhere, especially from priests and others who controlled temple wealth."[Ahmed 2006](#CITEREFAhmed2006), p. 1576: "Subsequently, hellenization of the elites transformed Anatolia into a largely Greek-speaking region"[McMahon & Steadman 2012a](#CITEREFMcMahonSteadman2012a), p. 5[McMahon 2012](#CITEREFMcMahon2012), p. 16[Sams 2012](#CITEREFSams2012), p. 617[Kaldellis 2024](#CITEREFKaldellis2024), p. 26

[^cite_note-17]: &#x20;[Davison 1990](#CITEREFDavison1990), pp. 3–4: "So the Seljuk sultanate was a successor state ruling part of the medieval Greek empire, and within it the process of Turkification of a previously Hellenized Anatolian population continued. That population must already have been of very mixed ancestry, deriving from ancient Hittite, Phrygian, Cappadocian, and other civilizations as well as Roman and Greek."[Howard 2016](#CITEREFHoward2016), pp. 33–44

[^cite_note-20]: &#x20;[Hanioğlu 2012](#CITEREFHanioğlu2012), pp. 15–25[Kayalı 2012](#CITEREFKayalı2012), pp. 26–28[Davison 1990](#CITEREFDavison1990), pp. 115–116

[^cite_note-21]: &#x20;[Kaser 2011](#CITEREFKaser2011), p. 336: "The emerging Christian nation states justified the prosecution of their Muslims by arguing that they were their former "suppressors". The historical balance: between about 1820 and 1920, millions of Muslim casualties and refugees back to the remaining Ottoman Empire had to be registered; estimations speak about 5 million casualties and the same number of displaced persons"[Fábos 2005](#CITEREFFábos2005), p. 437: "Muslims had been the majority in Anatolia, the Crimea, the Balkans, and the Caucasus and a plurality in southern Russia and sections of Romania. Most of these lands were within or contiguous with the Ottoman Empire. By 1923, 'only Anatolia, eastern Thrace, and a section of the southeastern Caucasus remained to the Muslim land \[...] Millions of Muslims, most of them Turks, had died; millions more had fled to what is today Turkey. Between 1821 and 1922, more than five million Muslims were driven from their lands. Five and one-half million Muslims died, some of them killed in wars, others perishing as refugees from starvation and disease' (McCarthy 1995, 1). Since people in the Ottoman Empire were classified by religion, Turks, Albanians, Bosnians, and all other Muslim groups were recognized—and recognized themselves—simply as Muslims. Hence, their persecution and forced migration is of central importance to an analysis of 'Muslim migration.'"Schayegh, Cyrus (2024). "A Late/Post-Imperial Region of Difference: The Ottoman Empire and its Successor Polities in Southeastern Europe, Turkey, and the Arab East, c. 1850s–1940s". *Journal of World History*. **35** (4): 579–622. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1353/jwh.2024.a943172](https://doi.org/10.1353%2Fjwh.2024.a943172). [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [1527-8050](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/1527-8050). Between 1821 and the 1919–1922 Turko-Greek War, about five and a half million Muslims died of religious-ethnic war-related causes, including disease and hunger during forced migration, in southeastern Europe and the Crimea and Caucasus.[Karpat 2001](#CITEREFKarpat2001), p. 343: "The main migrations started from Crimea in 1856 and were followed by those from the Caucasus and the Balkans in 1862 to 1878 and 1912 to 1916. These have continued to our day. The quantitative indicators cited in various sources show that during this period a total of about 7 million migrants from Crimea, the Caucasus, the Balkans, and the Mediterranean islands settled in Anatolia. These immigrants were overwhelmingly Muslim, except for a number of Jews who left their homes in the Balkans and Russia in order to live in the Ottoman lands. By the end of the century the immigrants and their descendants constituted some 30 to 40 percent of the total population of Anatolia, and in some western areas their percentage was even higher. \[...] The immigrants called themselves Muslims rather than Turks, although most of those from Bulgaria, Macedonia, and eastern Serbia descended from the Turkish Anatolian stock who settled in the Balkans in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries."[Karpat 2004](#CITEREFKarpat2004), pp. 5–6: "Migration was a major force in the social and cultural reconstruction of the Ottoman state in the nineteenth century. While some seven to nine million, mostly Muslim, refugees from lost territories in the Caucasus, Crimea, Balkans and Mediterranean islands migrated to Anatolia and Eastern Thrace, during the last quarter of the nineteenth and the early part of the twentieth centuries \[...]"[Pekesen 2012](#CITEREFPekesen2012): "The immigration had far-reaching social and political consequences for the Ottoman Empire and Turkey. \[...] Between 1821 and 1922, some 5.3 million Muslims migrated to the Empire.50 It is estimated that in 1923, the year the republic of Turkey was founded, about 25 per cent of the population came from immigrant families.51"[Biondich 2011](#CITEREFBiondich2011), p. 93: "The road from Berlin to Lausanne was littered with millions of casualties. In the period between 1878 and 1912, as many as two million Muslims emigrated voluntarily or involuntarily from the Balkans. When one adds those who were killed or expelled between 1912 and 1923, the number of Muslim casualties from the Balkan far exceeds three million. By 1923 fewer than one million remained in the Balkans"[Armour 2012](#CITEREFArmour2012), p. 213: "To top it all, the Empire was host to a steady stream of Muslim refugees. Russia between 1854 and 1876 expelled 1.4 million Crimean Tartars, and in the mid-1860s another 600,000 Circassians from the Caucasus. Their arrival produced further economic dislocation and expense."[Bosma, Lucassen & Oostindie 2012a](#CITEREFBosmaLucassenOostindie2012a), p. 17: "In total, many millions of Turks (or, more precisely, Muslim immigrants, including some from the Caucasus) were involved in this 'repatriation' – sometimes more than once in a lifetime – the last stage of which may have been the immigration of seven hundred thousand Turks from Bulgaria between 1940 and 1990. Most of these immigrants settled in urban north-western Anatolia. Today between a third and a quarter of the Republic's population are descendants of these Muslim immigrants, known as Muhacir or Göçmen"

[^cite_note-Horrocks_pp_778-779-98]: &#x20;[Horrocks 2008](#CITEREFHorrocks2008), pp. 778–779: "Thus the majority of traditional 'Greek' lands, including the coastal areas of Asia Minor, remained essentially Greek-speaking, despite the superimposition of Latin and the later Slavic incursions into the Balkans during the sixth and seventh centuries. Even on the Anatolian plateau, where Hellenic culture had come only with Alexander's conquests, both the extremely heterogeneous indigenous populations and immigrant groups (including Celts, Goths, Jews, and Persians) had become heavily Hellenized, as the steady decline in epigraphic evidence for the native languages and the great mass of public and private inscriptions in Greek demonstrate. Though the disappearance of these languages from the written record did not entail their immediate abandonment as spoken languages \[...]"

[^cite_note-101]: &#x20;[Lee 2023](#CITEREFLee2023), p. 4: "It should also be noted that even the early Turkic peoples, including the Tiele and the Türks, were made up of heterogeneous elements. Importantly, DNA studies demonstrate that the expansion process of the Turkic peoples involved the Turkicization of various non-Turkic-speaking groups. The "Turks" intermixed with and Turkicized various indigenous groups across Eurasia: Uralic hunter-gatherers in northern Eurasia; Mongolic nomads in Mongolia; Indo-European-speaking nomads and sedentary populations in Xinjiang, Transoxiana, Iran, Kazakhstan, and South Siberia; and Indo-European elements (the Byzantine subjects, among others) in Anatolia and the Balkans.11"[Findley 2005](#CITEREFFindley2005), p. 18: "Moreover, Turks do not all physically look alike. They never did. The Turks of Turkey are famous for their range of physical types. Given the Turks' ancient Inner Asian origins, it is easy to imagine that they once presented a uniform Mongoloid appearance. Such traits seem to be more characteristic in the eastern Turkic world; however, uniformity of type can never have prevailed there either. Archeological evidence indicates that Indo-Europeans, or certainly Europoid physical types, inhabited the oases of the Tarim basin and even parts of Mongolia in ancient times. In the Tarim basin, persistence of these former inhabitants' genes among the modern Uyghurs is both observable and scientifically demonstrable.32 Early Chinese sources describe the Kirghiz as blue-eyed and blond or red-haired. The genesis of Turkic ethnic groups from earliest times occurred in confederations of diverse peoples. As if to prove the point, the earliest surviving texts in Turkic languages are studded with terms from other languages."Golden, Peter B. (2018). "The Ethnogonic Tales of the Türks". *The Medieval History Journal*. **21** (2): 291–327. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1177/0971945818775373](https://doi.org/10.1177%2F0971945818775373). [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [0971-9458](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/0971-9458). [S2CID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S2CID_\(identifier\)) [166026934](https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID\:166026934). "Some DNA tests point to the Iranian connections of the Ashina and Ashide,133 highlighting further that the Turks as a whole 'were made up of heterogeneous and somatically dissimilar populations'.134 Geographically, the accounts cover the regions of Inner Mongolia, Gansu, Xinjiang, the Yenisei zone and the Altay, regions with Turkic, Indo-European (Iranian \[Saka] and Tokharian), Yeniseic, Uralic and other populations. Wusun elements, like most steppe polities of an ethno-linguistic mix, may have also played a substratal role."Lee, Joo-Yup; Kuang, Shuntu (2017). ["A Comparative Analysis of Chinese Historical Sources and Y-DNA Studies with Regard to the Early and Medieval Turkic Peoples"](https://doi.org/10.1163%2F22105018-12340089). *Inner Asia*. **19** (2): 197–239. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1163/22105018-12340089](https://doi.org/10.1163%2F22105018-12340089). [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [2210-5018](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/2210-5018). Both Chinese histories and modern dna studies indicate that the early and medieval Turkic peoples were made up of heterogeneous populations

[^cite_note-514]: &#x20;Tinaz, Cem; Turco, Douglas Michele; Salisbury, Paul (2 September 2014). "Sport policy in Turkey". *International Journal of Sport Policy and Politics*. **6** (3): 533–545. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1080/19406940.2014.893247](https://doi.org/10.1080%2F19406940.2014.893247). Without question, soccer is the most popular sport in Turkey and two former sports ministers (personal communication, 15–16 March 2012) go so far as to state that soccer represents probably the only sport or recognized sporting interest from a political perspective. Due to its profitability as a business, the media coverage of soccer is hugely significant, leading to debates over the non-coverage of other sports in the media in favour of soccer. However, newly established thematic sport channels are serving as an effective media for sports other than soccer. In recent years, basketball, volleyball and tennis have started to gain media coverage.
